What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I can’t really shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with something small, like a client not responding to an email, and then suddenly I’m thinking the whole contract is going to fall apart, and if that happens I’ll fall behind on my divorce payments, and then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again.\n\nI know that sounds like a lot from one email, but that’s where my head goes. I work in IT management, so there’s always some fire to put out, but lately it’s like my brain turns everything into the worst possible chain reaction.\n\nDoctor: Has something particularly stressful or traumatic happened to you recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t call it traumatic exactly, but the big thing was getting fired. That was about thirteen months ago now. And honestly, that’s when all of this really started.\n\nI did find contract work after that, so on paper it’s not like I’ve just been sitting around, but it never really let up. It’s funny, I used to tell people I was “between leadership roles,” like that made it sound strategic or something, but inside it just felt like I was constantly bracing for the next thing to go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most days since you got fired?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve had bad days, sure, especially after the firing, but “most days” is harder to say.\n\nIt’s more like I get stuck thinking about where I should be by now, and then I’ll remember someone I used to manage got promoted, or I’ll see some LinkedIn post—honestly LinkedIn is the worst for that—and then I’m off in my head comparing everything. But I’m not sure that’s the same as being depressed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Pretty much all the time, honestly.\n\nIt’s like I’m wound up even when nothing is actually happening. My shoulders are up here—like locked—and I’m waiting for bad news, even if I’m just sitting at my desk. I’ll catch myself pacing around the house between calls, or bouncing my leg during meetings, and then I realize no one has even said anything concerning.\n\nIt’s strange because in IT there’s always some alert or escalation, so part of me keeps telling myself, “Well, this is just the job,” but… this feels different. It’s like my body is already reacting before there’s a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do. Not always in an obvious “I’m avoiding this” way, but I’ll put things off because I start running through every possible way it could go badly.\n\nLike networking follow-ups—I’ll meet someone or someone will say, “Send me your info,” and then I just… don’t. I overthink the wording, whether I’ll seem desperate, whether they’ll think I’m not senior enough anymore, all of that. And with client deliverables, I’ve delayed sending things because I’m imagining every criticism before it’s even happened.\n\nAnd outside work, I’ve pulled back from soccer and seeing friends. Which is stupid, because those things used to help. But I get embarrassed by how tense I am, or I’m worried I’ll snap at someone or just be bad company. So I tell myself I’m busy, but really I’m avoiding it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety mostly about work-related things or does it also involve other areas of your life?\n\nPatient: It started with work, I think, or at least that’s the easiest place to point to. But it doesn’t stay there.\n\nMy mind jumps around—like one minute I’m thinking about contract work, then suddenly I’m calculating mortgage payments, then I’m wondering whether people in the Bethesda tech scene think I’m… I don’t know, damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter not emailing back right away, feels like this big sign that my whole career is closing down.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels connected. Work, money, reputation, the house—it all kind of becomes one big thing.\n\nDoctor: Is there ever a time when you feel completely free of these worries?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted, I guess, but completely free? No.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’ll start with one little thing, like a client not responding to an email, and then within minutes I’m off somewhere else entirely—thinking I’m going to lose the contract, then I’m behind on divorce payments, then I’ll never get back into a real leadership role again. And then I’m sitting there staring at my inbox like it’s going to tell me my whole future.\n\nIt sounds ridiculous when I lay it out like that, but it doesn’t feel ridiculous in my head. It feels like one thing automatically leads to the next.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or thing that usually triggers your anxiety, or does it feel like it can happen at any time?\n\nPatient: It can be a specific thing, but honestly it feels like almost anything can set it off.\n\nLike, a delayed email from a recruiter is a big one. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, they’re busy, calm down,” but then my brain turns it into proof that my whole career is closing down. And then I’m not just thinking about that email anymore—I’m thinking about contract work, mortgage payments, and whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as… damaged goods, I guess.\n\nIt jumps tracks really fast. I’ll start with one practical thing, and suddenly it’s this whole chain of everything that could go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific situation or problem, or is it more of a general feeling that something could go wrong at any time?\n\nPatient: It’s more the second one, I think. I mean, it can start with a specific thing, but it doesn’t stay specific.\n\nLike a recruiter takes too long to email me back, and suddenly that’s not just an email anymore. It turns into, “Okay, this means the contract work is drying up,” and then I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m wondering if people around Bethesda—tech people, former colleagues, whatever—think I’m damaged goods now.\n\nSo it’s not like I’m calmly worried about one problem. It feels more like anything can become the thing that proves everything is about to go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't worry about anything at all?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I wish I did.\n\nEven on a “quiet” day, my mind finds something to latch onto. It might start with something small, like a recruiter taking too long to answer an email, and then I’m off—thinking the contract work is going to dry up, then how I’m going to handle the mortgage, and then whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now.\n\nAnd I know you asked about a whole day with no worry, but… I don’t think I get those. It’s more like the topic changes, but the worrying is still there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious even when things are going well and there are no obvious problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually part of what’s so frustrating. Even if nothing is technically wrong—like there isn’t some emergency happening—my mind still starts scanning.\n\nI’ll be sitting there thinking, okay, things are fine today, and then a recruiter email is a little late, and suddenly it’s not just an email. It’s, “This means the contract work is drying up,” and then I’m thinking about the mortgage, and then somehow I’m back to wondering if people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods.\n\nAnd it’s weird because I can know, logically, that things are going okay in that moment, but my brain treats one small thing like proof my whole career is closing down.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble sleeping because of these worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI’ll get into bed and instead of shutting down, my brain starts replaying interview answers—like, “Why did I phrase it that way?” or “Did I sound too desperate?” And then I’m checking the clock at 2 or 3 in the morning, which of course makes it worse because then I’m doing the math on how little sleep I’ll get.\n\nIt reminds me of being on call for a system outage, weirdly—like even if nothing is happening, I’m still braced for the alert. So even when I technically get enough hours, I wake up feeling like I didn’t really rest.\n\nDoctor: Is there usually something specific that happens right before you start feeling anxious, or does the anxiety seem to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: Sometimes there is a specific little thing, yeah — like a recruiter taking too long to email me back. But it’s not like the thing matches the size of the reaction, if that makes sense.\n\nA delayed email turns into, “Okay, the contract work is drying up,” and then I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m off in this whole spiral about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. And Bethesda’s a weirdly small world for tech, or at least it feels that way when you’re in it long enough… people remember things, or I think they do.\n\nSo it can look like it starts with something specific, but honestly my mind is already kind of waiting for proof that everything is about to fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety follow you into situations that are typically relaxing or enjoyable for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. Even if I’m doing something that should be relaxing, it kind of comes with me.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to watch a movie or go out somewhere, and my mind jumps back to contract work—whether the next thing is coming in, whether a recruiter email taking too long means something bad. And then somehow I’m not even paying attention anymore because I’m thinking about the mortgage, and then that turns into, you know, “Do people in those Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now?”\n\nIt sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment a delayed email can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. So yeah, it’s hard for things to just be enjoyable.\n\nDoctor: Has your anxiety affected your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it has. I mean, I can still function, I guess, but not the way I used to.\n\nWith work, I’ve missed networking follow-ups—emails I know I should send, people I should circle back with—and then I put it off because I’m already imagining how awkward it’ll be or how they’ll read into it. And client deliverables too… I’ll delay them because I get stuck trying to anticipate every possible thing that could go wrong before I send something over.\n\nAnd relationships, yeah. I’ve kind of pulled back. I used to play soccer with some friends, and now I make excuses. Part of it is I’m embarrassed, honestly. I feel tense all the time, and I’m more irritable than I want to be, and I don’t want people seeing me like that. So I just… don’t show up as much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease significantly if your work situation and financial stability were to improve?\n\nPatient: I mean… probably some, yeah. If the work and money were steadier, I’m sure that would take some pressure off.\n\nBut I don’t know if it would just go away. My mind has this way of jumping tracks — contract work, then mortgage payments, then whether people around Bethesda tech circles are thinking I’m damaged goods. Like even one delayed email from a recruiter can start feeling like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nAnd then I’m thinking about people I haven’t talked to in years, like this one guy from a previous project who probably doesn’t even remember me the way I remember him, but in my head it’s all connected. So… yes, stability would help, but I think my brain would still find something to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually triggered by a specific event or situation, or does it feel more like a constant, general feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I guess, but more like it’s always running in the background. There are specific things that set it off, like if a recruiter doesn’t reply right away, then my mind goes straight to, “Okay, that means the work isn’t coming, the contract thing is drying up.”\n\nAnd then it jumps from that to the mortgage, and then somehow I’m thinking about people in the Bethesda tech circles and whether they all think I’m damaged goods now. Which, I know, that’s a lot to put on one email sitting unanswered, but in the moment it feels like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nSo it can start with a specific thing, but it doesn’t really stay contained there. It spreads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if the issues that started after you got fired, like your career and financial stability, were completely resolved?\n\nPatient: Completely? I don’t think so. I mean, it would help a lot if the career stuff and the money were stable, obviously.\n\nBut my mind doesn’t really stay in one lane. It’ll start with contract work, then suddenly I’m thinking about the mortgage, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. Like, a recruiter taking too long to answer an email can feel like… I don’t know, like proof that everything is collapsing, that my whole career is closing down.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it connects really fast. So if those issues were fixed, maybe it would quiet down some, but I’m not sure it would completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you were to find a stable and fulfilling job again?\n\nPatient: Completely go away? I want to say yes, because that would make sense, right? If I had a stable job again, something I felt good about, maybe I wouldn’t be so wound up all the time.\n\nBut I don’t know. My mind has this habit of… it doesn’t stop at the job. It jumps from contract work to the mortgage, then to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then one delayed email from a recruiter — which could mean nothing, people are busy — starts feeling like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nSo a good job would probably help. A lot, maybe. But I’m not sure it would shut the worry off completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease to a level where it doesn't interfere with your daily life if you were able to find a stable job and resolve your current financial concerns?\n\nPatient: I think it would go down, yeah. I mean, stable work and not worrying about the mortgage every second would take a big weight off.\n\nBut… I’m not sure it would stop interfering completely. Even now, it’s not just the money part. I’ll sit there trying to respond to a networking message or follow up with someone, and I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go badly, so then I don’t send it. Or with client deliverables, I’ve delayed things because I’m overthinking what might be wrong or how they’ll react.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve kind of pulled back too. I used to play soccer and see friends more, but I’ve been avoiding that because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I am. Like I don’t want to show up and be that guy who’s wound too tight over nothing. So… yes, a stable job would help, but I don’t know that it would make me normal again overnight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It spreads.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if the issues that started after you got fired, like your career and financial stability, were completely resolved?\n\nPatient: Completely? I don’t think so. I mean, it would help a lot if the career stuff and the money were stable, obviously.\n\nBut my mind doesn’t really stay in one lane. It’ll start with contract work, then suddenly I’m thinking about the mortgage, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods. Like, a recruiter taking too long to answer an email can feel like… I don’t know, like proof that everything is collapsing, that my whole career is closing down.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it connects really fast. So if those issues were fixed, maybe it would quiet down some, but I’m not sure it would completely go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would completely go away if you were to find a stable and fulfilling job again?\n\nPatient: Completely go away? I want to say yes, because that would make sense, right? If I had a stable job again, something I felt good about, maybe I wouldn’t be so wound up all the time.\n\nBut I don’t know. My mind has this habit of… it doesn’t stop at the job. It jumps from contract work to the mortgage, then to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods. And then one delayed email from a recruiter — which could mean nothing, people are busy — starts feeling like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nSo a good job would probably help. A lot, maybe. But I’m not sure it would shut the worry off completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would decrease to a level where it doesn't interfere with your daily life if you were able to find a stable job and resolve your current financial concerns?\n\nPatient: I think it would go down, yeah. I mean, stable work and not worrying about the mortgage every second would take a big weight off.\n\nBut… I’m not sure it would stop interfering completely. Even now, it’s not just the money part. I’ll sit there trying to respond to a networking message or follow up with someone, and I get stuck thinking through every possible way it could go badly, so then I don’t send it. Or with client deliverables, I’ve delayed things because I’m overthinking what might be wrong or how they’ll react.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve kind of pulled back too. I used to play soccer and see friends more, but I’ve been avoiding that because I’m embarrassed by how tense and irritable I am. Like I don’t want to show up and be that guy who’s wound too tight over nothing. So… yes, a stable job would help, but I don’t know that it would make me normal again overnight."
